# stands -- one worker per core -- but it stands on energy alone now rather
# than on the wall being nearly unchanged, and the README says so. This is
# the third figure this week that moved once it was taken again with its
# conditions written down, and the second whose stated reasoning had to be
# rewritten rather than just its digits.

# 2026-08-14, the tokenizer timed by a tool rather than by the shell. Three
# groups moved because benchmarks.adb gained a section. Not re-measured, and
# the reason is order: the new section runs after the row products, the
# device ratios and the share scaling, so it cannot have affected a figure
# taken before it started. Read Run to check that rather than take it on
# trust -- it is one call list, and the tokenizer lines sit between the
# vector kernels and the metadata parse.

# 2026-08-14, processor time from this repository's own tool. `tests speed`
# reports it beside the wall time, taken around the same region, which
# removes the one figure in this file that came from the shell. The reason
# it did not before -- that totalling processor time across worker tasks
# needs a host call bound per platform -- was wrong: it is the same file the
# load comes from.
#
# The twelve-token figure was retaken with it: 1.884 s and 10.39 s of
# processor time at a load of 1.28 to 1.82. Reference, device and drafting
# were not, and the reason is what that figure shows: the same measurement
# read 1.869 s before this change and 1.884 s after, which is inside the
# spread two consecutive runs of this tool have. What was added is two reads
# of one small file per repeat, one just inside each end of the timed
# region -- microseconds against seconds, and inside rather than outside
# because a processor time taken around a different region than the wall
# would answer about different work.
#
# The worker-count pair was not retaken either.
# Each half of that comparison needs its own quiet window and the machine
# gave one, not two; the numbers are kept and the README says they are the
# oldest here.

# 2026-08-14, the figures retaken with their loads. Three groups had none
# recorded and now have: the twelve-token figure, the reference comparison
# and the drafting trio. Every one of them moved, and by more than the
# change to the code between then and now could account for -- the
# twelve-token figure from 1.37 s to 1.87 s, the reference ratio from
# thirteen times to eleven, the drafted run from 4.094 s to 5.705 s.
#
# That is the argument for recording the load, made by the figures
# themselves. The older ones were taken on a machine whose state nobody
# wrote down, so there is no way to tell how much of the gap is the machine
# and how much is the program. From here on there is.
#
# What it cost: the tools refuse to measure above a load of 1.5, and this
# machine sat above it nearly all day. The runs waited for windows and took
# them one at a time.

# 2026-08-14, the busy-machine bound moved to Host_Load so that `tests speed`
# refuses on it too. Three groups moved for it. Not re-measured, and the
# reason is that nothing measured changed: the bound and the predicate moved
# from Benchmarks to Host_Load, benchmarks.adb calls them where it called
# them before, and the only other edit to that file is blank lines. The
# refusal runs before any timing.

# 2026-08-14, a device that stops answering gets its own code, and the bound
# gets an option. The engine is unchanged in what it computes; what moved is
# which diagnostic a stalled device produces and where the bound comes from.
#
# Re-measured, the short device run: 1.178 s at a load of 3.74, generating
# 1.000 s, against the published 1.262 and 1.062 at a load of 1.0. Faster on
# a busier machine, which is the noise this pair has always had and is why it
# is published beside its processor counterpart rather than alone. Not
# republished, for the reason given twice above.

# 2026-08-14, the wait made sayable. The slice and the whole bound are what
# the engine was opened for rather than constants, so a test can reach the
# giving-up path with a device in perfect health and can make a stop request
# land between slices instead of before the product starts.
#
# Re-measured, the short device run: 1.226 s at a load of 1.66 against the
# published 1.262 at 1.0, with generating at 1.053 against 1.062. Reading two
# fields off the engine instead of two constants costs nothing, which is what
# these say. Not republished, for the reason under the entry below: the pair
# is taken with its processor counterpart and replacing one column alone
# would make the comparison span two afternoons.

# 2026-08-14, the sliced wait and the token that reaches it. The device now
# waits for its fence in twenty-millisecond slices instead of one second, and
# asks between them whether the caller wants to stop.
#
# Re-measured, the device pair: 1.178 s short and 2.556 s long against the
# published 1.262 and 2.504, at a load of 1.85 to 2.17 against the published
# pair's 1.0. Slicing a wait costs nothing when the fence signals inside the
# first slice, which is what these numbers say. Not republished: the pair is
# published beside its processor counterpart and taken with it, and replacing
# one column with a run taken on its own would make the comparison a
# comparison of two different afternoons.
#
# The engine, reference and drafting groups were not re-measured. What they
# see of this change is one field assignment per Evaluate call -- the token
# for the call in progress, stored where the products can reach it -- and the
# device pair above, which measures the same assignment along with the part
# that actually changed, did not move.

# 2026-08-14, the second place the weights are freed. Repacking frees the
# arena while the model stays open, and the device was told about the model
# closing and not about that. Both go through one procedure now, which is
# the change these four fingerprints moved for.
#
# Re-measured rather than argued, for the group where the change is on a
# timed path: repacking happens during Prepare, and the twelve-token figure
# quotes loading. The pair reads 1.818 s and 1.407 s at a load of 1.25
# against the published 1.777 and 1.262 taken at 1.0 -- the generating
# component is 1.449 against 1.398 and 1.049 against 1.062, so the two runs
# agree to within what consecutive runs of this tool agree to. The published
# table is left alone: replacing figures with figures that say the same
# thing would lose the comparison with the run before, which is the only
# thing that makes either of them mean anything.
#
# The reference and drafting groups were not re-measured. The added call
# walks the list of matrices a device holds, which is empty on every
# processor path, and the engine group above measures the same code and did
# not move. That is the whole of the change: one procedure, called from the
# two places the weights are freed.

# 2026-08-14, the device forgetting a closed model's matrices. Four groups
# moved because llama.adb and the device engine changed. Not re-measured, and
# this is the reason: the whole change is one call in Model.Close and the
# procedure it calls. Close happens after every timed region these figures
# cover -- the twelve-token figure quotes load and generation separately and
# neither includes it, and the device pair is timed to the last token. The
# new procedure has no caller inside Multiply. The diff is five files and
# seventy-four added lines, all of them the procedure, its comment and the
# one call; read it rather than take this on trust.
#
# Worth recording why it was added: the conformance sweep was failing about
# half its runs, by a fifth of a logit, and moving from run to run. A device
# remembers a matrix by where its bytes lie, and the sweep closes a model per
# format and architecture with the device open across all of them, so the
# allocator handed the next model an address the device still had weights
# for. The comment in the shader-format entry below says the residency key
# was fixed for format and shape; this is the lifetime half of the same
# fault, and it was not fixed with it because the assumption written down at
# the time -- that in this program the weights live as long as the model --
# was true of the program and false of its own test sweep.

# 2026-08-14, the batch measured for every format too. It was one format at
# two widths, on the argument that a batch buys the same arithmetic
# everywhere -- and the argument was wrong. Batching compresses the spread
# across formats from nine to one down to two to one, and binary32 gains
# most of all: 1.39 with one vector, 0.26 with eight. An argument standing
# in for a measurement, in a file whose whole purpose is to stop that.
#
# Re-measured from one run at a load of 1.25: thirty device ratios and the
# row-product table, all published. The scaling group keeps the reason given
# under the entry below -- the change is again the device section's call
# list, and the share-scaling loop it covers is untouched by it.

# 2026-08-14, the benchmark measures every format on the device. It measured
# the three the shader used to decode; the other twelve had arrived as
# branches with nothing timing them.
#
# Re-measured from one run at a load of 1.29: the fifteen device ratios, the
# two batched ones and the row-product table, all published. The finding is
# that the device's advantage tracks how badly the processor's own decoder
# vectorizes -- four to one on Q5_0, Q5_1, IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS, which are the
# formats a per-lane shift or a gather makes slow on x86, and a quarter on
# the formats the processor is best at.
#
# The scaling group was not re-measured, and this is the reason. Its
# fingerprint moved because benchmarks.adb is in its source list and
# benchmarks.adb changed; the change is which formats the device section
# asks for, and the share-scaling loop it covers is not touched by it. Read
# the diff rather than take this on trust: it is one call list. The figures
# that group covers were taken pinned, which this run was not, so replacing
# them with unpinned numbers would be a worse measurement rather than a
# newer one.

# 2026-08-13, the shader learnt the other twelve formats. It decoded three of
# the fifteen and now decodes all of them, so nothing needs repacking to
# reach a device.
#
# Re-measured, everything this group covers, at a load of about 1.0 for the
# model runs and 1.29 for the benchmark: the four rows of the two-backend
# table, the three-way table of where the weights are, and the five per-format
# ratios. The long prompt on the device moved from 3.824 s to 2.504 s, which
# is not claimed as the change's doing -- the earlier pair was taken at a
# higher load. What the pair does say is that fifteen branches did not cost
# what three did: the ratios are within a few per cent of the run before,
# which is the risk that was worth measuring.
#
# The other three groups here changed because llama.adb, generation.adb,
# grammar.adb and cli-execute.adb were reindented -- blocks that had drifted
# three columns left of where they belong, which the compiler had been
# reporting as style warnings into log files nothing read. Not re-measured:
# whitespace outside a string literal is not in the generated code. Checked
# by reading each change rather than assumed, and they are all one edit:
# the body of a block moved right by three columns.

# 2026-08-13, the benchmark tool itself. Three of these groups take their
# figures from `tests benchmark` and none of them listed the tool. The same
# hole the speed_run note above describes, in the other measuring tool, and
# it opened the same way: nobody thought of the thing doing the measuring as
# a source. It closed on the change that made it matter -- the device rows
# now take the best of their rounds rather than the middle one, which moved
# three published ratios and would have moved them silently.
#
# Re-measured on a machine at 1.36 rather than the 2.34 of the previous run,
# which the tool now insists on: it refuses to measure at all above a load of
# 1.5. The five device ratios were republished and the single-vector rows
# moved by up to a sixth while the batched rows did not, which is the bias
# the README names, measured rather than argued. The row-product table was
# republished from the same run; nothing in it moved by more than eight per
# cent and the ordering is unchanged.
#
# The scaling group was not re-measured, and this is the reason. Its
# fingerprint moved because its source list gained a file, not because any
# source in it changed: benchmarks.adb is new to the list, and the only
# change inside it since these figures were taken is which round the device
# rows take. The scaling rows still take the median they always took, and
# Fastest has no other caller. The refusal above runs before any timing and
# the load reader runs outside every timed region.
#
# A pinned run was taken anyway, and it is worth recording that it read 10798
# Me/s for q8_0 at eight shares against the published 12420, on a machine at
# 1.38. That is thirteen per cent, in the direction a busy processor moves a
# processor figure, and it is why the figures this group covers are not
# replaced with it: they were taken under conditions this machine no longer
# offers, and a worse measurement is not a newer one. What it does say is
# that these figures predate the tool reporting a load, so the conditions
# they were taken under are not recorded anywhere -- unlike every figure
# taken from here on.
# load: 1.70 rising to 2.37, one `tests benchmark` run, 2026-08-15
# model: none. These figures time kernels on tensors this tool builds itself.
# 2026-08-21, every figure whose sources bert touched, re-measured. The
# architecture reaches the engine, the tokenizer and the device backend, so
# five of the seven groups went stale at once. What follows is what this
# machine gave back, at the loads each run reported, with the model the
# figures have always named.
#
# The processor did not move. Twelve tokens read 3.743 s against the 3.734 s
# the device table below was given earlier the same day on the code before
# bert, and the 110-token prompt 13.256 s against 13.353 s. Both runs
# reproduced their published digests -- 5abff916 short, cbf29ce4 long -- so
# the program says what it said. That is the answer to the question the
# fingerprints ask: the change did not cost the engine anything measurable.
#
#   engine     12 tokens, short prompt, cpu, 7 workers: 3.743 s wall,
#              0.466 s prompt, 3.215 s generating, 21.11 s of processor
#              time, load 0.75 to 1.45. The same run at 14 threads: 4.146 s
#              wall and 27.26 s of processor time, load 1.31 to 4.19 -- the
#              rise being the run filling the machine. Oversubscribing now
#              adds eleven per cent to the wall for twenty-nine per cent
#              more processor time, where on a quieter host it took
#              eighteen per cent off for fifty-five per cent more. The
#              advice that rested on it is turned round in the README.
#
#   reference  4 tokens: cpu 1.449 s wall (0.345 prompt, 1.104 generating)
#              against reference 12.822 s (7.712 and 5.110), both printing
#              5f85f064. Nine times the work in total, twenty-two on the
#              prompt and under five on the generation -- where the figures
#              this replaces read eleven, fifteen and eight. The ratio moved
#              because both ends moved, and neither end is code this change
#              touched.
#
#   device     short run 0.885 s against the processor's 3.743 s -- 0.122 s
#              of it evaluating the prompt against 0.466 s, and 0.627 s
#              generating against 3.215 s; long run 2.174 s against
#              13.256 s. Both faster than the 1.062 s and
#              2.631 s published, and the reason is not in the diff: one
#              push constant and a ternary were added to a shader whose
#              other branches are untouched. An integrated device shares a
#              fifteen-watt budget with the processor, so a quieter machine
#              hands it more, and these were taken quieter than those.
#
#   tokenizer  0.0139 s for sixty thousand ordinary characters and 0.0156 s
#              for sixty thousand brackets, against 0.0098 and 0.0127. The
#              hostile case still stays near the ordinary one, which is the
#              whole of what the pair is for; the WordPiece road added one
#              branch per call to Encode and none per character.
#
# 2026-08-21, drafting is not re-recorded, and this is why. The run gives
# 14.786 s with `proposed 24, accepted 9` where the table published
# `16 proposed, 9 accepted`; the generated text is byte-identical, digest
# 5abff916, so nothing about the answer changed. The count does not
# reconcile. Drafted accumulates Count - 1 a round and a round proposes at
# most the four --draft-tokens asks for, so 24 is six rounds; six rounds
# yield the target's own token each plus nine accepted, which is fifteen
# tokens from a run asked for twelve. The published 16 is four rounds and
# thirteen, which a truncated last round explains.
#
# Neither generation.adb, where that line lives, nor speed_run.adb, which
# reports it, is touched by the change that made this group stale. The last
# time this number was wrong the fault was in the tool and not the engine --
# 2fea96c, where it went from 20 and 11 to 16 and 9 because the tool read a
# prompt file the command reads differently.
#
# So this group is recorded under the second of the two answers this file
# allows: the change cannot have moved these figures. The drafting code is
# untouched, the tool that measures it is untouched, and the engine path
# both models evaluate through is unchanged -- which is not an assertion but
# the engine group above, where the same run on this machine reads what it
# read before bert. The row therefore keeps its published figures rather
# than taking a timing whose own proposal count does not add up, and the
# count is left here as a question somebody should chase with a run of its
# own rather than folded into a commit about an architecture.

# 2026-08-22, the fingerprints moved again and the figures did not. Reading
# a published all-MiniLM found two faults, and both are confined to code no
# figure here runs. The rotary width is now zero for bert by construction,
# which is a branch on the architecture and cannot reach a llama model; the
# WordPiece road looks a word's first piece up with its marker, which is a
# branch on the tokenizer kind and cannot reach the SentencePiece road the
# tokenizer figures are taken on. TinyLlama is what every timing above uses
# and it is neither.
#
# 2026-08-22, nomic-bert. The same again and for the same reason: a second
# architecture of Bert's shape reaches the engine and the tokenizer, and
# every branch it added is a branch a llama model does not take. What it
# changed in shared code is one allocation condition -- Post_Room is now
# asked of the arrangement rather than of a list -- and Gemma2, Gemma3,
# Falcon and Phi2 still take it exactly as they did. The engine figures were
# taken on this machine after the first of these architectures and read what
# they read before either.
#
# 2026-08-22, the drafting row is not going to be re-measured soon, and
# saying so is better than leaving the reader to wonder. The machine is
# carrying two other projects' runs for the foreseeable future and the tool
# refuses to publish a figure above a load of 1.50, rightly. What is known
# is above: the counts are self-consistent, the published pair has been
# stale since 6bea10f changed the drafting path six hours after the figures
# were taken, and the current truth is 24 proposed against 9 accepted. The
# row keeps its figures until somebody can take them properly.
#
# The same is true of the two KV-cache figures in the README, which quote
# the faster era of this machine beside an engine table that no longer does.
#
# 2026-08-22, two faults a second runtime found. Both are confined to code
# no figure here runs: the WordPiece road now wraps a text in its two
# markers, which is a branch on the tokenizer kind that the SentencePiece
# road the tokenizer figures are taken on does not take; and nomic-bert
# pairs its rotation the way every architecture but llama already did, which
# is a case arm for one architecture. TinyLlama is neither.
#
# Two more edits followed, and the same holds for both: a diagnostic that
# fires only where a text is longer than a context a causal model never
# reaches, and one metadata key read at load. Neither is in a timed path and
# neither is reachable by a llama model.
#
# So these are recorded under the second answer this file allows rather than
# measured a second time in one night: the change cannot have moved them.
# The engine figures above were taken after the first of the two edits and
# read what they read before bert, which is the evidence for the first half
# of that claim; the second half is a branch that a llama model does not
# take.

# load: 0.72 at the start of the pinned `tests benchmark` run this comes from, 2026-08-24
# 2026-08-25, taken again after four rows of the integer product went into
# one machine code insertion. The row-product table is the floating-point
# path and does not enter that kernel, and it says so: fourteen of fifteen
# rows are within a hundredth of a nanosecond of the last reading and the
# fifteenth, Q5_K, is 0.42 against 0.43. Q8_0 reads 0.40 against 0.39, Q5_0
# 0.56 against 0.55. Medians of three unpinned runs of `tests benchmark` on
# the kept kernel.
#
# What did move is the pair the same command reports for the two arithmetics,
# and re-reading it found a figure the README had left standing since before
# the byte dot product existed. The quantized path against the floating-point
# one is 4.44x with one vector a pass and 0.92x with thirty-two -- the second
# is below one. It is not this change: the same pair reads 4.44 and 0.92 on
# the compilation before it, alternated in one sitting, two rounds each.
# The batched case is where the floating-point path amortizes its decode over
# thirty-two vectors and the quantized one still has thirty-two activation
# vectors to round. That is the reading; the explanation is untested.
#
# Two things measured here and not taken.
#
# Eight lanes rather than four. The insertion folds the byte instruction's
# eight integer lanes into four with a vextracti128 and a vpaddd before it
# scales them. Keeping eight removes both: 65.4 thousand million instructions
# for the 110-token prompt against 71.96, and 1.022 s against 1.092 across
# three alternated rounds -- so it is faster, and by more than the noise
# floor. It changes what the model says: sixty-four generated tokens hash to
# e341d15aa6edc6a4 where the four-lane fold, both sixteen-bit compilations
# and every figure in the README say 1cb5fffbb21399ad. The fold makes each
# float accumulator receive an exact wider integer sum, and that is why the
# byte path agrees with the sixteen-bit one on this model today; the README
# says that agreement is not promised, and it is still worth more than six
# per cent here. Not taken, and the numbers are here so it can be reversed.
#
# An earlier reading of the same experiment said eight lanes were *worse*,
# and it was wrong: the lane array carried Alignment => 32 on sixteen bytes,
# GNAT padded every entry to thirty-two, and the insertion walks those
# entries by hand with a stride of sixteen. It read the wrong halves. The
# same defect appeared twice, an hour apart, the second time because a file
# was restored from HEAD and took the fix away with it. What caught it both
# times was the test comparing the three compilations, not the conformance
# sweep -- the sweep passed on the wrong answers, because a tolerance wide
# enough for a different rounding is wide enough for some wrong sums too.

# 2026-08-25, taken again after the prompt was given the strip kernel. The
# row-product table is the floating-point path and does not enter the integer
# kernel, and it says so again: every row within a hundredth of a nanosecond
# of the last reading. Q8_0 0.39 against 0.40, Q6_K 0.41 against 0.40, F16
# 0.59 against 0.58, Q5_1 0.59 against 0.60, Q2_K 0.75 against 0.74, the
# other ten unchanged. Read as a band, as the section says.
#
# The pair the same command reports for the two arithmetics: 4.42x the
# floating-point path with one vector a pass and 0.96 with thirty-two, where
# the sitting before read 4.44 and 0.92. The batched case is still below one
# and still unexplained past the guess recorded above it.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the weight scale was given VCVTPH2PS. The
# row-product table is the floating-point path and does not enter the integer
# kernel: every row within a hundredth of a nanosecond of the last reading,
# five of them moving by exactly that and ten not at all. F32 0.28 against
# 0.27, Q8_0 0.40 against 0.39, Q6_K 0.40 against 0.41, Q5_0 0.55 against
# 0.56, F16 0.58 against 0.59, Q5_1 0.60 against 0.59, Q2_K 0.76 against
# 0.75. Read as a band, as the section says.
#
# The pair for the two arithmetics did move, and only on the half that reads
# a scale per row rather than per row and vector: 4.71x the floating-point
# path with one vector a pass, against 4.42 before, and 0.96 with thirty-two,
# unchanged. That is the half-precision widening leaving the single-vector
# kernel, where it was a larger share.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after a tile of answers was written back in the
# order the target keeps them rather than the order the tile does.
#
# Almost nothing here moves, and that is the finding worth recording. The
# change is worth about four per cent of a prompt -- 0.777 s against 0.744,
# three rounds alternated in one sitting, and a profile that puts the
# procedure it touches at 2.1 per cent where it was 5.1 -- and the spread
# between one sitting and the next on this machine is larger than that. The
# 110-token prompt reads 0.782 s here against 0.784 published. **Alternated
# rounds are the instrument; a table taken a day apart is not.**
#
# Unrolling the strip kernel's block loop was measured in the same sitting
# and is not kept: 54.03 thousand million instructions against 54.79, and
# 0.756 s against 0.738 over three alternated rounds, better in none of
# them. The twenty-one per cent of samples a profile charged to that loop's

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant was given an integer
# kernel. Every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change,
# which is what these say: the 110-token prompt reads 0.806 s against 0.782,
# twelve tokens 0.458 s against 0.448, sixty-four generated 2.160 s against
# 2.165, and every digest is what it was. The spread between sittings is
# larger than any of those differences, as it has been all along.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   110-token prompt, floating-point path   4.598 s   31.67 s of processor
#   110-token prompt, byte dot product      1.317 s    8.19 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way        0.949 s / 0.930 s
#
# and the digest is 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six runs. The integer sums are
# exact either way and the rounding falls in the same places, which is not
# true of the eight-bit format: there the byte instruction groups a block's
# products differently and the README says so. Here it does not, because a
# nibble needs no bias and the minimum's term is taken out in binary64 at
# the end exactly as the floating-point path takes it.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.375 s for the prompt and 0.844 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The device figures are the noisiest rows here and both moved without
# anything touching them: the device's generating row reads 30.9 t/s against
# 27.3 last sitting, and llama.cpp at -ngl 0 reads 872.1 t/s against 774.6.
# Neither is a change to either program.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel of its own. As with the batch kernel before it, every figure
# in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.738 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.806 last sitting, 2.160 s for sixty-four
# generated against 2.160, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, floating-point path   0.938 s   6.33 s of processor
#   thirty-two generated, byte dot product      0.505 s   2.81 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                1.382 s / 1.442 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Fifty milliseconds a token against
# eighty-four, where the eight-bit format takes thirty-three.
#
# Why it was worth doing, which was not obvious and was measured before a
# line was written. The eight-bit format's generated token is bound by the
# memory path and stops getting faster at four workers, so no kernel can
# help it. This one was still getting faster at seven -- 1.510 s at two
# shares, 1.130 at four, 0.993 at seven -- because the floating-point path
# it was taking spends its time unpacking and multiplying rather than
# waiting. The sweep is the reason the work was done and would have been the
# reason not to.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.468 s for the prompt and 0.505 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The drafting row moved a long way and none of it is the draft: the check
# reads 120 ms against 72, on the same three commands and the same models,
# because the eight-bit model it checks for is measured through a
# twelve-token run whose wall is a third loading and warm-up. The conclusion
# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this
# acceptance a round yields less than the check costs, so no draft price
# pays.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant was given a kernel. As
# with the two before it, every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0
# path did not change: 0.719 s for the 110-token prompt against 0.738 last
# sitting, 2.187 s for sixty-four generated against 2.160, and every digest
# what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change, and which is three formats rather than one.
# Reading the file's tensor table: 135 tensors Q4_K, 21 Q6_K, 45 F32 norms;
# by element, 83.1 per cent Q4_K and 16.9 per cent Q6_K. A profile of the
# prompt before this change put accumulate_dot -- the floating-point row
# product, which by then meant Q6_K and nothing else -- at 44.7 per cent,
# beside the Q4_K kernel's 43.6. One sixth of the weights was costing half
# the time.
#
#   110-token prompt, Q6_K on the floating-point path   1.355 s   8.29 s cpu
#   110-token prompt, Q6_K on the byte product          0.990 s   5.47 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way                    0.508 / 0.501 s

# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Generating does not move because a

#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 0.994 s for the prompt and 0.508 s

# The drafting check reads 53 ms here against 72 and 120 in the two sittings
# before it, on unchanged code. It is a difference of two twelve-token runs
# and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up; the README now says
# to read it as tens of milliseconds rather than as a number.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every
# figure here is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.763 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.719, 2.159 s for sixty-four generated against
# 2.187, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the floating-point path   0.505 s
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the byte product          0.356 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                        1.017 / 0.983 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Thirty-five milliseconds a token where
# the eight-bit file takes thirty-three: a file two thirds the size now
# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s
#
# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.

kernels 77EB0DAB62549D76 src/library/model_runner-quantization.adb src/library/model_runner-numerics.adb src/library/model_runner-tensors.adb tests/src/benchmarks.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-decoders.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-wide.ads model_runner.gpr src/library/model_runner-byte_sources-files.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-plain.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-wide.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-deep.ads src/library/model_runner-platform-instructions.ads src/platform/linux/model_runner-platform-instructions.adb
